X-75][related_post_a366_x69_through_x75_leapfrogged_block].

**The X-76 carries two kinds of engine so that no one machine has to both lift it and push it fast.** Two turboshafts lift it, a turbofan pushes it, and between those two modes its proprotors stop, fold and disappear. **Every engineering number this article can derive about the aeroplane follows from that division**, and the most useful of them is that the turbofan's certificated thrust sets a ceiling on how much drag the whole aircraft may carry at 400 knots, a ceiling that under the simplest model of how thrust falls with altitude is the same at every altitude.

The X-76A designation was allocated on 20 October 2025 to Bell Textron for the Speed and Runway Independent Technologies programme, SPRINT, of the Defense Advanced Research Projects Agency, which is DARPA. Its row has an engine cell reading `2 GE CT7-8, PW308C+`, a sponsor cell reading DARPA, and a description that the register marks as the compiler's wording rather than the Department of Defense's \[[DOD 4120.15-L Addendum][ref_mds_addendum]].

> Unmanned demonstrator for *SPRINT* (Speed and Runway Independent Technologies) program.

**At this article's date of 15 December 2025 that row was not public.** It first appears in an archived copy of the register between 15 January and 1 February 2026 \[[DOD 4120.15-L Addendum, as archived on 15 January 2026][ref_mds_addendum_wb_2026_01]\] \[[DOD 4120.15-L Addendum, as archived on 1 February 2026][ref_mds_addendum_wb_2026_02]\], and DARPA announced the designation on 9 March 2026 \[[DARPA on the X-76][ref_darpa_x76]\]. **The programme behind it was public, and in unusual depth.** The solicitation of March 2023 is on the federal contracting portal with its proposers' day slides, three budget justification books inside the date carry a programme entry, the federal award record holds three of the four first-phase contracts, and Bell's own patents describe stop-fold mechanisms of the kind the aircraft needs. **So this article knows the aeroplane's envelope from the government's own documents and its engines from the register, and it knows almost nothing else.** The designation itself, and the founding-year reading of its number, belong to the [X-69 through X-75][related_post_a366_x69_through_x75_leapfrogged_block] article and are summarised here only where the aeroplane needs them.

**The genre test places this in the full order and not the reduced one**, because a vehicle exists and was being built after its critical design review. **It has not flown and will not fly inside the dateline**, so it is documentation-poor, and every section that would describe flight describes instead what the government asked for and what physics allows.

## The Research Question

**The programme states its own question, and the solicitation and DARPA's programme page state it in the same numbers.** The solicitation asks for technologies that let an aircraft "cruise at speeds from 400 to 450 Knots, hover in austere environments, and transition between these modes of operation in a stable and time critical manner" \[[SPRINT broad agency announcement][ref_sprint_baa]\]. DARPA's programme page, as it stood three days before this article's date, gives the same pair of numbers, cruise from 400 to 450 knots at relevant altitudes and hover in austere environments from unprepared surfaces \[[DARPA SPRINT programme page, as archived on 12 December 2025][ref_darpa_sprint_wb_2025_12]\].

**The question is therefore runway independence at jet cruise speed, and it is hard because the two halves want opposite rotors.** Hover wants a large disk moving a large mass of air slowly, since the ideal power to hover falls as the square root of the disk area grows. High speed wants the opposite, a small, stiff propulsor whose blade tips stay subsonic when the aircraft's own speed is added to their rotation. **A rotor big enough to hover efficiently is too big to cruise at 450 knots, and a propulsor fit for 450 knots is too small to hover efficiently.** Bell's own paper on the concept states the same tension in one sentence, that hover requires significant power that can only be mitigated by larger diameter rotors, but that large diameter rotors become a detriment to achieving transonic flight \[[Schank et al 2025][research_schank_xin_2025]\].

### Two Propulsion Systems, Each Sized by One Flight Phase

**The stop-fold answer is to stop asking one propulsor to do both jobs.** The proprotors lift the aircraft in hover and pull it through conversion like those of any tiltrotor. At some airspeed the wing carries the weight, a separate jet takes over the thrust, and the proprotors are feathered, stopped, and folded back along their nacelles. **The register's engine cell is the physical form of that answer**, two turboshafts and a turbofan. Engle and Sambell described the configuration in 1969 in terms that still fit, the rotors stopped in axial flow in a 120 to 200 knot range and the blades folded into streamlined pods, so that "the lift and propulsion systems can thus be optimized for their respective operating regimes" \[[Engle and Sambell 1969][research_engle_sambell_1969]\].

**This article's keystone is that sentence made quantitative.** If each system is sized by one phase, then the turboshafts' certificated power bounds what the aircraft can hover and the turbofan's certificated thrust bounds what it can push at 400 knots. Neither bound needs the aircraft's mass, wing or rotor diameter, which are not public, to be stated as an envelope. **The sizing section derives both envelopes**, and the most useful result is that the turbofan's sea-level rating, with an assumed lapse factor, fixes the largest equivalent drag area the aircraft can carry at 400 knots, at every altitude in the solicitation's band.

### Why a Proprotor Cannot Simply Be Driven Faster

**A conventional tiltrotor runs out of speed for two reasons that this article treats, and both are on the proprotor.** The first is aeroelastic. A large, flexible proprotor on a flexible wing can couple its gyroscopic precession to the wing's bending and torsion and go unstable, the instability called whirl flutter, and the speed at which it occurs falls as the wing is made thinner and less draggy \[[Acree et al 1999][research_acree_johnson_1999]\] \[[Johnson 1974][research_johnson_1974]\]. **The second is compressibility.** A blade tip sees the aircraft's forward speed and its own rotational speed combined, so the helical tip Mach number rises with airspeed even when the rotor turns no faster. The sizing section quantifies this second limit from the XV-15's published rotor speed. **The stop-fold configuration escapes both limits at once by removing the rotor from the airstream**, which is exactly what a 1972 full-scale wind tunnel test reported, the stopped rotor eliminating the rotor, pylon and wing instability \[[The History of the XV-15 Tilt Rotor Research Aircraft][ref_xv15_history]\].

**So the research question is not whether a rotor can be stopped in flight, which was shown at full scale in a wind tunnel in 1972.** It is whether an aircraft built around that idea, with existing engines and a flight control system that sequences the transition, can hover over an unprepared surface and then cruise at 400 knots, which no aircraft in any record read for this article has done.

## The Register Row, and What Its Engine Cell Says

**The row has five cells and only the description is marked as the compiler's.** The date, the designation, the maker, the engines and the sponsor carry no mark and are official data by the register's own scheme, while the whole description cell carries the class the compiler uses for wording that is not from Department records \[[DOD 4120.15-L Addendum][ref_mds_addendum]\]. **The engines are therefore the only official configuration fact about this aeroplane in the public record**, and they are engines of two kinds, two turboshafts and, on the reading taken below, one turbofan.

**Two General Electric CT7-8 turboshafts are a helicopter powerplant.** The CT7-8 series is the commercial member of the family whose military members are the T700 engines, the New Zealand regulator's acceptance report recording that "the original CT7-1 turboshaft was a commercial derivative of the military T700 engine" \[[New Zealand type acceptance report for the CT7 series][ref_nz_ct7]\] and General Electric calling the line "the family of T700/CT7 turboshaft engines" \[[General Electric on the certification of new CT7-8 models, 2004][ref_ge_ct7_2004]\]. **The same register holds 23 other rows powered by T700 or CT7 variants**, and 20 of them are helicopters, two are the HC-144 transports with turboprop CT7s, and one is the [X-49][related_post_a346_piasecki_x49], a compound helicopter. **One PW308C is a business-jet turbofan** of about 31 kilonewtons \[[Type-certificate data sheet for the PW308 series][ref_easa_pw308]\], which the New Zealand regulator's acceptance report says "was developed for the Falcon 2000EX aircraft and is rated at 7002 lb. maximum takeoff thrust" \[[New Zealand type acceptance report for the PW308 series][ref_nz_pw308]\]. **The two documents agree to within a pound**, the European sheet's 31.15 kilonewtons being 7,003 pounds. **No other row in the register pairs a turboshaft with a turbofan**, every other cell naming more than one kind of engine belonging to a missile or a target, and that pairing is what a stop-fold aircraft is.

### The Plus Sign After the Turbofan Has Four Readings

**The cell reads `2 GE CT7-8, PW308C+`, and the trailing plus sign is not explained anywhere in the register.** This article does not resolve it. It records the four readings that the evidence allows and says what each would mean.

**The first reading is that the plus is part of a model name, and the register supports it.** Two other rows end in a plus, the BQM-167A and the BQM-177A targets, both powered by a `Microturbo Tri 60-5+` \[[DOD 4120.15-L Addendum][ref_mds_addendum]\], and the Air Force's own fact sheet for the first gives its power plant as "1 MicroTurbo Tri 60-5+", so in those rows the plus is part of the engine's name \[[Air Force BQM-167A fact sheet, as archived on 11 November 2025][ref_af_bqm167]\]. **Where the register combines two kinds of engine, a count and a second engine always follow the plus**, as in `1 XJ400-CA-400 + 1 Solid Propellant` and `1 Mark 104 Mod 3+ 1 Mark 72 Mod 1`, and in this row nothing follows it.

**The second reading is an improved PW308C.** Pratt and Whitney Canada announced an enhanced PW308C in June 2011 for the Falcon 2000S, with changes to the combustor and fuel delivery \[[AviTrader on the enhanced PW308C][ref_avitrader_pw308c]\]. **A compiler or a source document might mark that variant with a plus**, but no document read here does.

**The third reading is the solicitation's engine rule.** The solicitation requires existing engines and permits off-the-shelf engines with minor upgrades that make no change to the engine core \[[SPRINT proposers day slides][ref_sprint_overview]\], and DARPA's answers to proposers say that "modifications that include the engine core are not allowed" \[[SPRINT questions and answers][ref_sprint_qa]\]. **A plus could mark such an upgrade.** That is an inference from the rule's existence and from nothing in the row.

**The fourth reading is truncation.** The plus could be the start of a combination whose second term was lost, an electrical machine or an auxiliary system for example. **The register's own combination syntax makes this the least likely of the four**, since every combination it prints continues after the plus with a count and a name.

**Which engine count governs the turbofan is the same kind of question.** The count 2 precedes the CT7-8 and nothing precedes the PW308C. The register's other multi-type rows give a count for each type, so **the cell read literally says two turboshafts and an unstated number of turbofans, and this article takes the turbofan count as one.** That reading agrees with the published renderings. DARPA's own artist's concept, released on 9 March 2026 and so outside this article's date, shows one lit exhaust at the tail between two canted fins, with the folded blades lying along each wingtip nacelle \[[DARPA artist's concept of the X-76][ref_darpa_x76_image]\], and a journalist's description of the renderings says the same \[[The Aviationist on the X-76][ref_aviationist_x76]\]. **An artist's concept is not an engineering drawing**, and the reading rests on the register cell first and the picture second. The count matters, because the certificated PW308C is approved for multiple-engine installation only, which is a dependent-systems point taken up below.

### The Description Is the Compiler's and It Says Unmanned

**The description calls the X-76 an unmanned demonstrator, and that word is not official.** The solicitation explicitly left crewing to the proposer, saying that "the decision to have the SPRINT X-Plane as manned, unmanned, optionally piloted, etc. is left to the proposer" \[[SPRINT broad agency announcement][ref_sprint_baa]\]. **Two documents inside the date are consistent with an uncrewed aircraft without saying so.** The solicitation's slides anticipate that "a flight termination system may be required if unmanned" \[[SPRINT proposers day slides][ref_sprint_overview]\], and the fiscal year 2026 budget book plans "ground station and human-machine interface development" \[[DARPA budget justification, fiscal year 2026][ref_darpa_pb2026]\]. **A ground station is what an uncrewed aircraft is flown from**, so the plans support the compiler's word, as an inference. After the date, DARPA released one rendering of the X-76 that shows no cockpit glazing \[[DARPA artist's concept of the X-76][ref_darpa_x76_image]\] and that journalists read as uncrewed and a second of a future operational aircraft described as optionally piloted \[[The War Zone on the X-76][ref_twz_x76]\] \[[DARPA SPRINT programme page][ref_darpa_sprint]\].

### The Row Was Not Public at This Article's Date

**Three archived copies of the register bracket the row's first public appearance.** The copy of 10 December 2025 does not contain it \[[DOD 4120.15-L Addendum, as archived on 10 December 2025][ref_mds_addendum_wb_2025_12]\], nor does the copy of 15 January 2026, and the copy of 1 February 2026 does. **So the row became public between 31 and 48 days after this article's date**, and 87 to 104 days after the allocation it records. Writing $t\_{\mathrm{a}}$ for the allocation, $t\_{\mathrm{d}}$ for this article's date, $t\_{\mathrm{pub}}$ for the row's first public appearance and $t\_{\mathrm{DARPA}}$ for the announcement,

$$
t_{\mathrm{pub}} - t_{\mathrm{d}} \in [\,31,\ 48\,]\ \text{days}, \qquad t_{\mathrm{pub}} - t_{\mathrm{a}} \in [\,87,\ 104\,]\ \text{days}, \qquad t_{\mathrm{DARPA}} - t_{\mathrm{d}} = 84\ \text{days}.
$$

The compiler's note on the row, read in the current copy, quotes DARPA's statement that the out-of-sequence number is "a deliberate nod to the revolutionary spirit of 1776", which is 84 days after this article's date. **This article uses the row as a fact about the aeroplane and dates every use of it.** The [X-69 through X-75][related_post_a366_x69_through_x75_leapfrogged_block] article measured this lag and treats the number's meaning, and nothing here revisits either.

## Programme Origin, From the Solicitation to the Critical Design Review

**The programme's history can be told almost entirely from government documents**, which is unusual for a vehicle that has not flown, and the documents disagree with each other and with the contractor in ways worth recording.

### The Solicitation Set the Aeroplane's Envelope Before Any Design Existed

**DARPA's Tactical Technology Office posted solicitation HR001123S0031 on Friday 10 March 2023**, held a proposers' day on 23 March, took abstracts until 31 March and proposals until 5 May, and expected to announce awards in September with work beginning in October \[[SPRINT proposers day slides][ref_sprint_overview]\]. **The programme was joint from the first slide**, with Headquarters, United States Special Operations Command, and its Special Operations Forces Acquisition, Technology and Logistics Center as partner, and the contracting officer's representative to be that center's deputy programme manager. **The programme manager at the proposers' day was Dr. Andrew Baker.** The programme page by December 2024 named Ian Higgins \[[DARPA SPRINT programme page, as archived on 24 December 2024][ref_darpa_sprint_wb_2024_12]\], and the announcement of March 2026 gives his rank as commander, United States Navy.

**The solicitation asked for two designs, not one.** Each proposer had to size an Operational System to a representative mission and a Demonstrator X-Plane traceable to it, and only the demonstrator was to be built \[[SPRINT broad agency announcement][ref_sprint_baa]\]. **The operational mission is specific.** It carries a 5,000 pound payload in a notional cargo bay 30 feet long, 8 feet wide and 7 feet high, hovers out of ground effect at sea level on a 102 degree Fahrenheit day for take-off and at 4,000 feet on a 95 degree day mid-mission and for landing, flies two legs of 350 nautical miles at best-range speed, and dashes at maximum continuous power at 400 knots true airspeed as a threshold and 450 as an objective \[[SPRINT proposers day slides][ref_sprint_overview]\].

**The demonstrator guidance is the closest thing to a specification of the X-76 that is public.** It reads as follows in the slides.

| Attribute | Guidance |
|---|---|
| Gross take-off weight | 8,000 to 15,000 pounds, "weight range is not limiting" |
| Sustained speed | at least 400 knots true airspeed between 15,000 and 30,000 feet |
| Useful payload | at least 1,000 pounds, mainly for flight test instrumentation |
| Flight test endurance | 1.5 hours |
| Radius | at least 200 nautical miles |
| Load margins | minus 0.5 to plus 2 g |
| Design life | 500 hours, with DARPA's demonstration expected to use 200 or fewer |
| Landing | 12 feet per second sink rate with no damage |

**Three further rules shaped the configuration as much as any number.** The X-plane had to use existing engines, and the government would not supply them. **There was no disk loading, downwash or surface hardness limit**, the proposer being asked instead to explain how the design meets the goal of runway independence. **And the goal was first flight of the demonstrator no more than 42 months from contract award** \[[SPRINT broad agency announcement][ref_sprint_baa]\]. The solicitation budgeted Phase 1A at the order of 15 million dollars, with 75 million dollars available for Phase 1 in total, and it allowed either a procurement contract or an other transaction. **The answers DARPA posted on 30 March 2023 sharpen four of these rules** \[[SPRINT questions and answers][ref_sprint_qa]\]. The Phase 1 money was 15 million dollars for Phase 1A and 60 million for Phase 1B, "a total split among performers", which is the solicitation's 75 million,

$$
15 + 60 = 75\ \text{million dollars}.
$$

Engine modifications were allowed, but "modifications that include the engine core are not allowed". A proposer could switch instruments between phases, an other transaction for Phase 1 and a regulation-based contract for Phase 2 being given as the example. **And "the SPRINT X-Plane is not intended to transition to an operational system"**, which the budget books, describing a demonstrator that transitions for further risk reduction flight testing, do not contradict, since that transition is of the test aircraft and not to production.

### Thirteen Offers and Four Awards, Three of Them in the Contract Record

**The federal award record holds three first-phase contracts and states that each was competed with thirteen offers received.** All three are cost-plus-fixed-fee research contracts under the solicitation's number, described as Speed and Runway Independent Technologies, and the award notices posted to the contracting portal on 2 and 7 November 2023 \[[Award HR001124C0325 to Aurora Flight Sciences][ref_award_aurora]\] \[[Award HR001124C0326 to Piasecki Aircraft][ref_award_piasecki]\] \[[Award HR001124C0327 to Northrop Grumman][ref_award_northrop]\] \[[Award notice for HR001124C0326][ref_sam_notice_0326]\] \[[Award notice for HR001124C0325][ref_sam_notice_0325]\] \[[Award notice for HR001124C0327][ref_sam_notice_0327]\].

| Award | Recipient | Start | Obligated to date |
|---|---|---|---:|
| HR001124C0326 | Piasecki Aircraft | 31 October 2023 | 4,944,700 dollars |
| HR001124C0325 | Aurora Flight Sciences | 1 November 2023 | 33,301,718 dollars |
| HR001124C0327 | Northrop Grumman | 1 November 2023 | 5,406,094 dollars |

**DARPA's programme page says the programme entered Phase 1 on 1 November 2023**, which agrees with two of the three start dates \[[DARPA SPRINT programme page, as archived on 12 December 2025][ref_darpa_sprint_wb_2025_12]\]. Bell announced its own selection for Phase 1A on 27 November 2023 \[[Bell selected for SPRINT Phase 1A][ref_bell_phase1a]\]. **The Piasecki and Northrop Grumman contracts' periods of performance end on 30 April 2024**, which is where the record shows their participation stopping.

### Bell's Instrument Is Not in the Contract Record

**No contract, grant or indefinite-delivery vehicle from DARPA to any Bell entity for this programme appears in the award record**, searched by programme name, by the phrases speed and runway and runway independent, and by recipient. **The solicitation allowed an other transaction, its answers allowed a different instrument in each phase, and other transactions are generally not reported as procurement contracts**, so the absence is consistent with Bell holding one. **That is an inference.** No document read here names Bell's instrument, its value or its date, and the article's statements about money are confined to what the record holds.

### Phase 1B, and the Money the Record Allows Bell

**Aurora's contract carries both phases, and its modifications date the Phase 1B decision.** The obligations before 24 May 2024 total 4,843,311 dollars, and modification P00006 on 24 May 2024 obligates 12,974,331 dollars, after which a further 15,484,076 dollars follow through February 2025 \[[Award HR001124C0325 to Aurora Flight Sciences][ref_award_aurora]\]. **That date agrees with DARPA's statement that Aurora and Bell were awarded Phase 1B in May 2024** and with Bell's release of 28 May 2024 \[[Bell awarded funding for SPRINT Phase 1B][ref_bell_phase1b]\]. The contract's total decomposes exactly into the three periods,

$$
4{,}843{,}311 + 12{,}974{,}331 + 15{,}484{,}076 = 33{,}301{,}718\ \text{dollars}.
$$


**The three recorded Phase 1A obligations sum to 15,194,105 dollars**, Aurora's pre-option figure with Piasecki's and Northrop Grumman's totals,

$$
4{,}843{,}311 + 4{,}944{,}700 + 5{,}406{,}094 = 15{,}194{,}105 = 15{,}000{,}000 + 194{,}105\ \text{dollars},
$$

**which already exceeds the solicitation's Phase 1A figure by 194,105 dollars before Bell's share is counted.** So the planning figure for Phase 1A was not a cap on what was obligated, and a figure that was not a cap for one sub-phase is weak evidence of a cap for the phase. **The three recorded contracts together total 43,652,512 dollars**, and if the solicitation's 75 million dollars for Phase 1 had held as a ceiling,

$$
B_{\mathrm{Bell}} \le 75{,}000{,}000 - (33{,}301{,}718 + 4{,}944{,}700 + 5{,}406{,}094) = 31{,}347{,}488\ \text{dollars}.
$$

**Bell's Phase 1 instrument could then not have exceeded 31,347,488 dollars, but the Phase 1A overrun shows that the premise of that bound did not hold for the first sub-phase**, so it is stated as the bound the planning figure implies and not as an estimate of what Bell received.

**Aurora's contract has one more feature that this article records without interpreting.** Its last modification, on 27 August 2025, carries the description `DARPA RESEARCH PROJECT` in place of the programme's name, and its period of performance ends on 21 October 2025, which is the day after the X-76A allocation.

### The Other Finalist Also Chose Two Kinds of Engine

**Aurora's own releases describe the design Bell beat, and it shares the X-76's propulsion answer while differing in everything else.** Aurora's release of 20 May 2024 describes a "low-drag, fan-in-wing demonstrator that integrates a blended wing body platform" with three lift fans and "an uncrewed cockpit", capable of 450 knots and using "existing engine solutions" \[[Aurora on its SPRINT design, May 2024][ref_aurora_may24]\]. **Its release of 8 October 2024 is more specific.** The demonstrator was uncrewed, with a 45-foot wingspan and a 1,000 pound payload, and "the propulsion system includes off-the-shelf turbofan and turboshaft engines that would power the vehicle to a maximum of 450 knots true airspeed" \[[Aurora on its SPRINT design, October 2024][ref_aurora_oct24]\].

**So both Phase 1B designs carried a turbofan and a turboshaft.** One buried its lift fans in a blended wing and the other folded its rotors, and both answered the solicitation's existing-engine rule with two kinds of engine rather than one convertible machine. **That is the strongest evidence in the record that the separate-engine choice belongs to the rule rather than to Bell**, and it is evidence from the competitor's documents. The October 2024 release also gives the schedule as it then stood, with preliminary design review "scheduled for April 2025", the phase running "through May 2025", and "flight testing for the program is planned for 2027".

### Phase 2 Began in May, June or July 2025, Depending on Who Is Asked

**Three sources inside or near the date give three months for the same decision.** DARPA's programme page says that in June 2025 Bell "was awarded a contract for Phases 2 and 3" \[[DARPA SPRINT programme page, as archived on 12 December 2025][ref_darpa_sprint_wb_2025_12]\]. Bell announced its selection for Phase 2 on 9 July 2025 \[[Bell to build the SPRINT X-plane][ref_bell_phase2]\]. **DARPA's announcement of March 2026 says the programme began its second phase in May 2025 "following the downselect to Bell"** \[[DARPA on the X-76][ref_darpa_x76]\]. These can all be true at once, a selection in May, an award in June and an announcement in July, but **no document read here says which event each date names**, and the article does not choose.

**The contractor's account of what Phase 2 includes changed between its own releases.** The release of July 2025 describes Phase 2 as "design, construction, ground testing and certification of an X-plane demonstrator", while the solicitation had placed Phase 2 as critical design, build and ground test and Phase 3 as flight test. **The certification in that sentence is airworthiness certification for an experimental aircraft**, which the solicitation also placed in Phase 2, following a DARPA flight release tailored from the airworthiness handbook MIL-HDBK-516C.

### Two Contractor Datelines Are a Year Early on One Mirror

**Textron's investor-relations copies of two Bell releases carry datelines a year earlier than the releases themselves.** The Phase 1B release, listed under 2024 and describing events of late 2023, is datelined "May 28, 2023" in its body \[[Textron copy of the Phase 1B release][ref_textron_phase1b]\]. The Phase 2 release, listed under 2025, is datelined "July 9, 2024" \[[Textron copy of the Phase 2 release][ref_textron_phase2]\]. **Bell's own newsroom carries the correct dates**, 28 May 2024 for the first and 9 July 2025 for the second, so the error belongs to the mirror and not to the contractor's record. **It is recorded because a reader who took either dateline from the mirror would place Phase 1B before Phase 1A existed**, and a chronology built from mirrored releases would be wrong by exactly a year in two places.

### The Schedule Has Moved by at Least Eight Months

**The solicitation's goal of first flight within 42 months of contract award puts first flight by 1 May 2027 for an award on 1 November 2023.** The slides' notional schedule draws first flight in fiscal year 2027 for each notional performer \[[SPRINT proposers day slides][ref_sprint_overview]\]. **As late as October 2024 the programme planned flight testing for 2027**, by Aurora's account of the schedule it was working to \[[Aurora on its SPRINT design, October 2024][ref_aurora_oct24]\]. **By July 2025 it planned flight testing in 2028**, so the slip arose between those two dates, across the preliminary design review. Inside this article's date, a DARPA spokesperson told Breaking Defense in July 2025 that the agency expected a completed demonstrator in 2027 and flight testing in 2028 \[[Breaking Defense on the Phase 2 selection][ref_bd_phase2]\], and DARPA's March 2026 announcement, outside it, says early 2028. **Taking early 2028 as 1 January 2028, the slip is at least eight months, and first flight would come at least 50 months after the Phase 1 award.** Counting calendar months from the award,

$$
t_{\mathrm{ff}}^{\mathrm{goal}} = t_{\mathrm{award}} + 42\ \text{months} = 1\ \text{May}\ 2027, \qquad t_{\mathrm{ff}}^{\mathrm{plan}} - t_{\mathrm{ff}}^{\mathrm{goal}} \ge 8\ \text{months}, \qquad t_{\mathrm{ff}}^{\mathrm{plan}} - t_{\mathrm{award}} \ge 50\ \text{months},
$$

and the 42-month goal is 1,277 days. **The programme's other intervals set the pace against which that slip is measured**, 236 days from the solicitation's posting to the first award, and 719 days from that award to the designation's allocation,

$$
t_{\mathrm{award}} - t_{\mathrm{BAA}} = 236\ \text{days}, \qquad t_{\mathrm{a}} - t_{\mathrm{award}} = 719\ \text{days}.
$$

That is a statement about a goal and a plan, both of which are documents, and not about any engineering difficulty, which no document read here describes.

## What Four Budget Books Said

**DARPA's research, development, test and evaluation budget justification books carry a programme entry for this programme in fiscal years 2024 through 2027**, three of them inside this article's date and the fourth, of April 2026, outside it \[[DARPA budget justification, fiscal year 2024][ref_darpa_pb2024]\] \[[DARPA budget justification, fiscal year 2025][ref_darpa_pb2025]\] \[[DARPA budget justification, fiscal year 2026][ref_darpa_pb2026]\] \[[DARPA budget justification, fiscal year 2027][ref_darpa_pb2027]\]. **No earlier book mentions it**, the only matches for the programme's acronym in the fiscal year 2021 book belonging to swarm sprints in an unrelated programme. The entry sits in programme element 0603286E, Advanced Aerospace Systems, until fiscal year 2026, when it moves to programme element 0603468E, Advanced Complex Systems, project ACX-01, along with other programmes of the same project, among them the X-planes of the [X-65][related_post_a362_aurora_x65_crane] and [X-68][related_post_a365_general_atomics_x68_longshot] articles.

### The Same Year Reported Three Ways

**Each book restates the earlier years, and the restatements are large.** Figures are millions of dollars.

| Book for fiscal year, and its date | 2024 | 2025 | 2026 | 2027 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 2024, March 2023 | 22.663 | | | |
| 2025, March 2024 | 22.663 | 36.866 | | |
| 2026, June 2025 | 13.024 | 41.998 | 55.200 | |
| 2027, April 2026, outside the date | | 47.000 | 47.524 | 62.113 |

**Fiscal year 2024 was requested at 22.663 million and executed at 13.024 million**, a shortfall of 9.639 million in the programme's first year. **Fiscal year 2025 grew from a request of 36.866 million to an actual of 47.000 million in the latest book**, about 27.5 percent more. **Fiscal year 2026 was requested at 55.200 million and the latest book carries 47.524 million**, about 13.9 percent less, though that figure is the current-year column of the latest book rather than an execution. **The latest figures for fiscal years 2024 through 2027 sum to 169.661 million dollars.** In millions,

$$
22.663 - 13.024 = 9.639, \qquad \frac{47.000}{36.866} - 1 = 0.275, \qquad 1 - \frac{47.524}{55.200} = 0.139, \qquad 13.024 + 47.000 + 47.524 + 62.113 = 169.661.
$$

None of these figures is a contract value, and none of them can be reconciled with the award record's 43.65 million without Bell's missing instrument.

### A Scaled Demonstrator Became a Demonstrator

**The fiscal year 2024 book describes a programme that "culminates in the fabrication and flight test of a scaled demonstrator."** The fiscal year 2025 book, a year later, drops the word scaled, and every later book follows it. The solicitation itself had asked for "a scaled X-Plane demonstrator to validate the enabling technologies at a relevant size". **This article reads the change as vocabulary rather than concept**, since the demonstrator guidance of 8,000 to 15,000 pounds was already a scale relative to the operational system and nothing in the plans changes, but it records the change because the word scaled is the only statement in any budget book about the aircraft's size relative to anything.

### The Transition Partner Changed Its Name Four Times

**Each book names who the demonstrator transitions to, and each names it differently.** The fiscal year 2024 book says "military service members", the 2025 book "military services", the 2026 book "a DoD component" and the 2027 book "a Services component". **The 2027 book also rewrites the missions**, adding contested logistics under attack and electronic warfare and dropping logistics support and the sentence that "the thresholds for speed and range have evolved with military strategy and mission needs". **Special Operations Command is not named anywhere in the programme's entries in any book**, its only appearance being the books' tables of contents, although it is named as the joint partner on every DARPA page and in the solicitation.

### The Plans Describe an Aircraft Flown From a Ground Station

**The plans lists are the closest thing to a schedule the books hold, and they read in sequence.** The 2024 book plans conceptual design review "for multiple concepts". The 2025 book plans preliminary design review and the start of limited detailed and critical design. **The 2026 book, dated June 2025 and inside the date, plans the subsystems by name**, "propulsion system, thermal management system, hydraulics, electrical system", along with control law development, engineering drawing release, airworthiness certification, integration with the flight test facility, manufacture of the demonstrator, and "ground station and human-machine interface development" \[[DARPA budget justification, fiscal year 2026][ref_darpa_pb2026]\]. **The 2027 book, outside the date, adds a structural proof load test, final software integration laboratory testing with simulated failures, and ground checkouts "including engine runs"** \[[DARPA budget justification, fiscal year 2027][ref_darpa_pb2027]\].

**A thermal management system appears by name in the subsystem list.** This article cannot say why, and records only that an aircraft carrying at least three gas turbines, two of which may idle or shut down in cruise, has more than one heat source to manage.

## Sizing From First Principles

**Nothing about the X-76's mass, wing, rotor diameter or drag is public at this article's date, nor in any later document read for it.** What is public is the solicitation's envelope and the certificated ratings of the engines the register names, taken as two turboshafts and one turbofan. **So this section does not estimate the aeroplane.** It derives what those engines can do for any aeroplane inside the envelope, which bounds the X-76 from both ends.

**Every input is one of three kinds and the text says which.** Engine ratings and masses are certificated figures from the type-certificate data sheets. The weight range, speed band and altitude band are the solicitation's. **Everything else is a named assumption, swept where a result depends on it**, and the assumptions are the hover figure of merit, the transmission efficiency, the wing download, the thrust lapse factor, the helical tip Mach limit and the maximum lift coefficient. Results that depend on an assumption say so where they are stated.

### The Symbols This Article Uses

| Symbol | Meaning | Unit |
|---|---|---|
| $W$ | gross weight | N |
| $m\_0$ | gross mass, with $W = m\_0 g$ | kg |
| $g$ | standard gravitational acceleration, 9.80665 | m/s² |
| $m\_e$ | dry mass of the three engines together | kg |
| $\mu\_e$ | engine mass fraction, $m\_e / m\_0$ | none |
| $P$ | installed shaft power of the two turboshafts at a stated rating | W |
| $P\_h$ | shaft power required to hover out of ground effect | W |
| $P\_F$ | thrust power of the turbofan, $F V$ | W |
| $\mathrm{FM}$ | rotor figure of merit in hover, assumed 0.75 | none |
| $\eta$ | transmission and accessory efficiency in hover, assumed 0.90 | none |
| $d$ | wing download as a fraction of weight, assumed 0.10 | none |
| $A$ | total disk area of the two proprotors | m² |
| $w$ | disk loading, $W / A$ | Pa |
| $r$ | radius of one proprotor | m |
| $v\_h$ | induced velocity at the disk in hover | m/s |
| $\rho$ | air density | kg/m³ |
| $\rho\_0$ | sea-level standard density, 1.225 | kg/m³ |
| $\sigma$ | density ratio, $\rho / \rho\_0$ | none |
| $T$ | air temperature | K |
| $T\_0$ | sea-level standard temperature, 288.15 | K |
| $p$ | air pressure | Pa |
| $p\_0$ | sea-level standard pressure, 101,325 | Pa |
| $\Lambda$ | tropospheric temperature lapse rate, 0.0065 | K/m |
| $h$ | geopotential altitude | m |
| $R$ | specific gas constant of air, 287.05 | J/(kg·K) |
| $\gamma$ | ratio of specific heats of air, 1.4 | none |
| $a$ | speed of sound | m/s |
| $F$ | turbofan thrust at altitude and speed | N |
| $F\_0$ | turbofan sea-level static thrust at a stated rating | N |
| $\varphi$ | the turbofan's thrust lapse factor at cruise Mach number, swept 0.5 to 0.7 | none |
| $q$ | dynamic pressure, $\tfrac{1}{2} \rho V^2$ | Pa |
| $V$ | true airspeed | m/s |
| $f$ | equivalent flat-plate drag area | m² |
| $L$, $D$ | lift and drag | N |
| $M$ | flight Mach number, $V / a$ | none |
| $M\_h$ | helical tip Mach number of a proprotor in axial flight | none |
| $\Omega$ | proprotor rotational speed | rad/s |
| $V\_t$ | proprotor tip speed, $\Omega r$ | m/s |
| $V\_s$ | wing stall speed | m/s |
| $S$ | wing reference area | m² |
| $C\_L$ | lift coefficient, with $C\_{L}^{\max}$ its maximum, assumed 1.6 | none |
| $E$ | lift-to-drag ratio, $L / D$ | none |
| $q\_w$ | dynamic pressure of the fully developed hover wake | Pa |
| $\kappa$ | proprotor tip speed as a fraction of the XV-15's airplane-mode tip speed | none |
| $\lambda$ | proprotor advance ratio in axial flight, $V / V\_t$ | none |
| $N\_e$, $N\_r$ | turboshaft output speed and proprotor speed | rev/min |
| $\mathcal{G}$ | speed reduction from turboshaft output to proprotor, $N\_e / N\_r$ | none |
| $n\_F$ | number of turbofans fitted | none |
| $t$ | a date, with $t\_{\mathrm{a}}$ the allocation, $t\_{\mathrm{d}}$ this article's date, $t\_{\mathrm{pub}}$ the row's first public appearance, $t\_{\mathrm{DARPA}}$ the announcement, $t\_{\mathrm{BAA}}$ the solicitation, $t\_{\mathrm{award}}$ the first Phase 1A award and $t\_{\mathrm{ff}}$ first flight, goal or plan | date |
| $B\_{\mathrm{Bell}}$ | the value of Bell's Phase 1 instrument | dollars |
| $\bar{c}$ | mean number of literature clusters per admitted record | none |

### The Atmosphere

**Every density and speed of sound below comes from the standard troposphere**, the 1976 standard's constants applied with the hydrostatic relation \[[U.S. Standard Atmosphere, 1976][ref_atm76]\]. Below 11 kilometres,

$$
T = T_0 - \Lambda h, \qquad p = p_0 \left( \frac{T}{T_0} \right)^{g / (\Lambda R)}, \qquad \rho = \frac{p}{R T}, \qquad a = \sqrt{\gamma R T}.
$$

**The solicitation's hover condition is 4,000 feet on a 95 degree Fahrenheit day**, specified by pressure altitude and temperature. Holding pressure on the standard profile and raising temperature to 308.15 kelvin gives a density of 0.9893 kilograms per cubic metre, a density ratio of 0.8076. **Since induced power at fixed weight and disk area scales as the inverse square root of density**, the hot-day condition costs

$$
\sqrt{\frac{\rho_0}{\rho_{\mathrm{hot}}}} = \sqrt{\frac{1.225}{0.9893}} = 1.113,
$$

an 11.3 percent increase in induced power, before any loss of engine power with temperature, which this article does not model. **The turboshafts' own hot-day lapse is not in any document read here**, so every hot-day result below is optimistic by that unmodelled amount.

### The Installed Power and What It Hovers

**The certificated CT7-8 delivers 1,879 kilowatts, 2,520 shaft horsepower, at its five-minute take-off rating and 1,523 kilowatts at maximum continuous, with a dry mass of 243.6 kilograms** \[[Type-certificate data sheet for the CT7 series][ref_easa_ct7]\]. The register names the model as `CT7-8` without a suffix. **The same data sheet lists the suffixed CT7-8 models from 1,845 to 2,009 kilowatts at take-off**, so if the register's name stands for the family rather than the base model, the installed power lies between about 2 percent below and 7 percent above the base figure,

$$
\frac{1845}{1879} = 0.982, \qquad \frac{2009}{1879} = 1.069.
$$

Two engines give

$$
P = 2 \times 1879\ \text{kW} = 3758\ \text{kW}
$$

at take-off and 3,046 kilowatts at maximum continuous.

**Momentum theory gives the power to hover in terms of disk loading alone.** An ideal rotor producing thrust $W(1+d)$ through a disk of area $A$ needs an induced power equal to that thrust times the induced velocity, and a real one needs that power divided by its figure of merit and by the drive system's efficiency. With $w = W / A$,

$$
P_h = \frac{W (1 + d)^{3/2}}{\mathrm{FM}\, \eta} \sqrt{\frac{w}{2 \rho}}.
$$

**Setting $P\_h$ equal to the installed power and solving for weight gives the heaviest aeroplane the two turboshafts can hover at a chosen disk loading**,

$$
W_{\max} = \frac{P\, \mathrm{FM}\, \eta}{(1 + d)^{3/2}} \sqrt{\frac{2 \rho}{w}},
$$

and solving instead for disk loading at the solicitation's heaviest weight gives the highest disk loading at which that weight can still hover,

$$
w_{\max} = 2 \rho \left( \frac{P\, \mathrm{FM}\, \eta}{W (1 + d)^{3/2}} \right)^{2}.
$$

**These depend on the three hover assumptions, and the table states them at the assumed values.** None of the three is taken from a measurement of the X-76 or quoted from a report. The figure of merit of full-scale tiltrotor proprotors was measured at Ames Research Center's outdoor facility in the 1980s, for the XV-15's metal and composite blades and a model of the V-22's \[[Felker et al 1986][research_felker_maisel_1986]\] \[[Felker and Betzina 1985][research_felker_betzina_1985]\], and the magnitude of the wing download for typical tiltrotor configurations is the subject of a review of that research \[[Felker 1988][research_felker_1988]\]. **This article read those reports' abstracts and one report's text, and it does not quote a figure from them**, because the measured values sit in plotted and tabulated data whose extracted text is not reliable. The assumed values are typical of that literature's subject and are labelled as assumptions throughout. Weights are in pounds, disk loadings in pounds per square foot, and the power fraction is the share of the two engines' take-off power needed to hover 15,000 pounds at sea level.

| Disk loading | Heaviest hover weight, sea level | Heaviest hover weight, 4,000 ft and 95 °F | Power fraction at 15,000 lb | Radius of each rotor at 15,000 lb |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 10 | 35,358 | 31,775 | 0.424 | 4.71 m |
| 13.2, the XV-15's | 30,775 | 27,657 | 0.487 | 4.10 m |
| 15 | 28,870 | 25,944 | 0.520 | 3.85 m |
| 20 | 25,002 | 22,469 | 0.600 | 3.33 m |
| 30 | 20,414 | 18,345 | 0.735 | 2.72 m |
| 60 | 14,435 | 12,972 | 1.039 | 1.92 m |

**The XV-15's disk loading of 13.2 pounds per square foot is its published figure, at a design gross weight of 13,000 pounds on two 25-foot proprotors** \[[The History of the XV-15 Tilt Rotor Research Aircraft][ref_xv15_history]\]. Each rotor's radius follows from the total disk area shared by two equal rotors,

$$
r = \sqrt{\frac{W}{2 \pi w}}.
$$

**Two CT7-8s hover 15,000 pounds at sea level at any disk loading up to about 55.6 pounds per square foot**, which corresponds to rotors of about 4.0 metres diameter each. Since the limit is proportional to density, on the solicitation's hot day before engine lapse it falls to

$$
w_{\max}^{\mathrm{hot}} = \sigma_{\mathrm{hot}}\, w_{\max} = 0.8076 \times 55.6\ \text{psf} = 44.9\ \text{psf}.
$$

**At the XV-15's disk loading they would hover about 30,800 pounds at sea level**, twice the solicitation's heaviest figure,

$$
\frac{W_{\max}(w = 13.2\ \text{psf})}{15{,}000\ \text{lb}} = \frac{30{,}775}{15{,}000} = 2.05.
$$

**Every hot-day weight in the table is the same fraction of its sea-level neighbour**, because the heaviest hover weight scales as the square root of density at fixed power and disk loading,

$$
\frac{W_{\max}(\rho_{\mathrm{hot}})}{W_{\max}(\rho_0)} = \sqrt{\frac{\rho_{\mathrm{hot}}}{\rho_0}} = \sqrt{0.8076} = 0.899,
$$

which is the hot day's 10 percent penalty on hover weight before any engine lapse. **So the turboshafts are not the constraint on the X-76's weight at any conventional tiltrotor disk loading.** Either the aircraft is heavier than the solicitation's guidance, which the guidance explicitly permits, or it hovers with a large power margin, or it hovers at a disk loading well above a conventional tiltrotor's, and the engines alone cannot say which. **Two observations favour the third.** Folded blades must lie along their nacelles, and Bell's patent on a stowed-blade clamp describes a blade "foldable to a stowed position extending substantially parallel to the pylon" \[[US 2021/0078695][ref_pat_us20210078695]\], so blade length is bounded by nacelle length. And the solicitation set no disk loading limit. **Both are inferences about the X-76, not facts about it.**

### Disk Loading Is the Price of Runway Independence

**The solicitation asks for hover over unprepared surfaces with personnel nearby and then declines to limit disk loading, downwash or surface hardness**, asking the proposer to explain how the design meets the goal instead \[[SPRINT broad agency announcement][ref_sprint_baa]\]. **Momentum theory makes the trade explicit.** The induced velocity at the disk is

$$
v_h = \sqrt{\frac{(1 + d)\, w}{2 \rho}},
$$

and the fully developed wake below an ideal rotor moves at twice that. At sea level, the XV-15's disk loading gives 16.8 metres per second at the disk and 33.7 in the wake. **A disk loading of 20 gives 20.7 and 41.5, and a disk loading of 60 gives 35.9 and 71.8.** The wake speed rises with the square root of disk loading. **What the ground and a person standing on it feel is the wake's dynamic pressure, and that has a cleaner form**, since squaring twice the induced velocity cancels the density,

$$
q_w = \tfrac{1}{2} \rho \left( 2 v_h \right)^2 = \tfrac{1}{2} \rho \cdot \frac{4 (1 + d)\, w}{2 \rho} = (1 + d)\, w.
$$

**The fully developed wake's dynamic pressure equals the rotor's thrust per unit disk area, at any altitude and on any day.** At the XV-15's disk loading it is 695 pascals, 14.5 pounds per square foot, at a disk loading of 20 it is 1,053 pascals, and at 60 it is 3,160 pascals, 66 pounds per square foot. So **a stop-fold aircraft that shortens its blades to stow them buys cruise speed with downwash in exact proportion**, which is the quantity runway independence is about. The literature on outwash and its effect on people standing near a hovering aircraft is cited in the survey below, and one of its studies is titled for that question \[[Calvert and Wenren 2019][research_calvert_wenren_2019_2]\], which this article cites for its title only.

### The Jet Is a Drag Budget

**The certificated PW308C produces 31.15 kilonewtons at its five-minute take-off rating and 31.13 kilonewtons at maximum continuous, at sea-level static conditions, with a dry mass of 623.5 kilograms** \[[Type-certificate data sheet for the PW308 series][ref_easa_pw308]\]. **How that thrust falls with altitude and speed is not in the data sheet or any document read here.** This article therefore models it in the simplest form consistent with turbofan behaviour, thrust falling with density at a fixed Mach factor,

$$
F = F_0\, \sigma\, \varphi,
$$

and sweeps the factor $\varphi$ from 0.5 to 0.7 at cruise Mach numbers. **That range is an assumption, not a measurement of this engine**, and the results are stated across it.

**In steady level flight thrust equals drag.** Writing the aircraft's total drag as the dynamic pressure times an equivalent flat-plate area, $D = q f$, and noting that the thrust available is an upper bound on the drag that can be carried,

$$
f_{\max} = \frac{F}{q} = \frac{F_0\, \sigma\, \varphi}{\tfrac{1}{2} \sigma \rho_0 V^2} = \frac{2 F_0\, \varphi}{\rho_0 V^2}.
$$

**The density ratio cancels, so the largest drag area the jet can push at a given true airspeed is the same at every altitude**, to the extent that the lapse factor is. At 400 knots true airspeed the coefficient $2 F\_0 / (\rho\_0 V^2)$ is 1.200 square metres and at 450 knots it is 0.948. **Across the swept lapse factor, the X-76 can carry at most 0.60 to 0.84 square metres of equivalent drag area at 400 knots, 6.5 to 9.0 square feet, and 0.47 to 0.66 square metres at 450 knots.** That budget includes the induced drag of lifting the aircraft, so the parasite budget is smaller still. **It must hold the fuselage, the wing, two nacelles with their folded blades, the tails and the inlet**, which is why the folded configuration has to be clean, and why Bell has patented a chined spinner and blade fairings for the folded rotor, the abstract describing blades "folded down" in forward flight against fairings that cover them \[[US 12,030,623][ref_pat_us12030623]\].

**The same model gives the lift-to-drag ratio the aircraft must reach.** Thrust equal to drag and lift equal to weight require

$$
\left( \frac{L}{D} \right)_{\mathrm{req}} = \frac{W}{F} = \frac{W}{F_0\, \sigma\, \varphi}.
$$

At 15,000 pounds and the middle of the lapse range, the required ratio is 5.68 at 15,000 feet, 6.70 at 20,000, 7.97 at 25,000 and 9.55 at 30,000. At 8,000 pounds it is 3.03 at 15,000 feet and 5.09 at 30,000. **The requirement rises with altitude because thrust falls with density while weight does not**, so the bottom of the solicitation's altitude band is where the turbofan most easily meets the speed requirement, and **a heavy X-76 would most plausibly demonstrate 400 knots near 15,000 feet.** That is an inference from a model. **Turned around, the same relation gives the highest altitude at which a given lift-to-drag ratio still allows 400 knots**, since level flight needs the density ratio to be at least

$$
\sigma_{\min} = \frac{W}{F_0\, \varphi\, E},
$$

which at 15,000 pounds and the middle of the lapse range is 0.595 for $E = 6$, 0.447 for $E = 8$ and 0.357 for $E = 10$, standard-atmosphere altitudes of about 16,700, 25,100 and 31,200 feet. **A lift-to-drag ratio near 6 in the folded cruise configuration would confine a 15,000 pound X-76 to the bottom of the solicitation's band**, and the band's top needs a ratio near 10.

**The turbofan's thrust power is comparable to the turboshafts' shaft power.** At 400 knots and 15,000 feet, at the middle of the lapse range,

$$
P_F = F V = 31.13\ \text{kN} \times 0.6292 \times 0.6 \times 205.8\ \text{m/s} = 2.42\ \text{MW},
$$

against 3.05 megawatts of maximum continuous shaft power from the two CT7-8s,

$$
\frac{P_F}{P_{\mathrm{mcp}}} = \frac{2.42\ \text{MW}}{3.05\ \text{MW}} = 0.79.
$$

**So the aircraft carries two power sources of the same order and uses one at a time**, which is the cost of having two propulsion systems, and it is why Bell's patents describe drive systems that let an otherwise idle lift engine contribute thrust in cruise through a pylon-mounted ducted fan \[[US 11,325,719][ref_pat_us11325719]\], or that couple and decouple rotors and thrust fans through clutches in one gearbox \[[US 11,577,831][ref_pat_us11577831]\]. **Whether the X-76 does either is not public.**

### The Proprotor's Speed Limit, and Why the Rotor Must Stop

**A proprotor in axial flight sees its blade tip moving at its own tip speed and the aircraft's speed at right angles**, so the helical tip Mach number, the variable against which the advanced high-speed propeller programme measured its noise \[[Dittmar and Hall 1991][research_dittmar_hall_1991]\], is

$$
M_h = \frac{\sqrt{V^2 + V_t^2}}{a}.
$$

**The XV-15 turned its 25-foot proprotors at 517 revolutions per minute in airplane mode and 589 in helicopter mode**, tip speeds of 206.3 and 235.0 metres per second \[[The History of the XV-15 Tilt Rotor Research Aircraft][ref_xv15_history]\]. **The hover figure can be checked against a measurement.** At sea level 235.0 metres per second is a tip Mach number of 0.69, inside the range of 0.60 to 0.73 over which a full-scale XV-15 rotor's hover performance was measured, a range across which that test found "very little effect on rotor performance due to tip Mach number variations" \[[Felker and Betzina 1985][research_felker_betzina_1985]\]. Its height-velocity envelope in the same appendix reaches about 300 knots true at 16,000 feet, where its airplane-mode helical tip Mach number is 0.80. **At the X-76's speeds the same tip speed is no longer workable.** At 400 knots and 15,000 feet it gives 0.90, and at 450 knots and 25,000 feet it gives 1.00, a tip that is sonic.

**Holding the helical tip Mach number to a limit fixes the largest tip speed a proprotor may turn at**,

$$
V_t \le a \sqrt{M_{h,\max}^2 - M^2},
$$

and taking 0.85 as that limit, which is an assumption, the XV-15's airplane-mode tip speed is exceeded above 350 knots at 15,000 feet and above 318 knots at 25,000. **To hold the limit at 450 knots and 25,000 feet the rotor would have to turn at about 61 percent of that tip speed**, and at 400 knots and 15,000 feet at about 88 percent. **And at an airspeed equal to the limit times the speed of sound no tip speed at all satisfies it**,

$$
V_{\mathrm{wall}} = M_{h,\max}\, a,
$$

which is 532 knots at 15,000 feet and 512 knots at 25,000. **The objective of 450 knots is therefore not beyond the compressibility wall**, but it is in the region where a proprotor must be slowed so far that its advance ratio is high and its thrust per unit of tip speed is poor. **The two quantities make that concrete.** The tip speed allowed as a fraction of the XV-15's, and the advance ratio that results, are

$$
\kappa = \frac{a \sqrt{M_{h,\max}^2 - M^2}}{V_t^{\mathrm{XV15}}}, \qquad \lambda = \frac{V}{\kappa\, V_t^{\mathrm{XV15}}},
$$

which give $\kappa = 0.877$ and $\lambda = 1.14$ at 400 knots and 15,000 feet, and $\kappa = 0.607$ and $\lambda = 1.85$ at 450 knots and 25,000 feet. **The XV-15 at its own 300 knots ran at an advance ratio of 0.75**, so the X-76's objective asks a proprotor for two and a half times the advance ratio of the aircraft that proved the configuration. That, with whirl flutter, is the case for stopping the rotor rather than slowing it. **Bell's paper states the conclusion and not this arithmetic**, that the stop-fold configuration can "make the rotor disappear" in cruise \[[Schank et al 2025][research_schank_xin_2025]\].

### The Conversion Must Happen Where the Wing Can Fly

**The rotor can only be stopped once the wing is carrying the whole weight and the jet the whole drag.** The lower bound on the speed at which that is possible is the wing's stall speed,

$$
V_s = \sqrt{\frac{2\, (W/S)}{\rho_0\, C_{L}^{\max}}},
$$

which at sea level, taking a maximum lift coefficient of 1.6, is 105 knots at a wing loading of 60 pounds per square foot, 122 at 80 and 136 at 100. **The wing loading of the X-76 is not public and those three values are illustrative.** The historical literature puts the stopping speed in the range that result implies. Engle and Sambell placed rotor stopping "in the 120 to 200-knot speed range" in 1969 \[[Engle and Sambell 1969][research_engle_sambell_1969]\], and the full-scale folding rotor tested in 1972 completed more than forty start and stop sequences at tunnel speeds up to 175 knots, making "smooth start/stop transitions" in about two seconds \[[Report CR-114464 1972][research_reportcr114464_1972]\]. **The conversion corridor of a stop-fold aircraft is therefore a band bounded below by the wing, at the illustrative stall speeds between 105 and 136 knots, and above by the loads on a rotor being stopped and on blades being folded in a moving airstream, which the historical tests put between 175 and 200 knots**, and a stopped blade at the wrong angle can go unstable in a way a turning blade does not, a full-scale test having found "violent dynamic instabilities" of a stopped blade that its author concluded were a type of stall flutter \[[White 1969][research_white_1969]\].

### The Engines Alone Are a Sixth of the Heaviest Aeroplane

**On the one-turbofan reading, the three engines' dry masses sum to 1,110.7 kilograms, 2,449 pounds**, 487.2 kilograms of turboshaft and 623.5 of turbofan,

$$
m_e = 2 \times 243.6 + 623.5 = 1110.7\ \text{kg}.
$$

As a fraction of gross mass,

$$
\mu_e = \frac{m_e}{m_0} = \frac{1110.7\ \text{kg}}{6803.9\ \text{kg}} = 0.163 \quad \text{at 15,000 lb}, \qquad \frac{1110.7\ \text{kg}}{3628.7\ \text{kg}} = 0.306 \quad \text{at 8,000 lb}.
$$

**Bare engines at 16 percent of gross mass, before transmissions, shafts, the fold mechanism, inlets and nacelles, put the X-76 at the heavy end of the solicitation's range or above it.** At the light end the bare engines alone would be almost a third of the aircraft. **This is the second line of evidence, after the hover power margin, that the demonstrator is heavier than 15,000 pounds or close to it**, and both are inferences from certificated engine data and not statements about the aircraft. The turboshafts' specific power and the turbofan's static thrust-to-weight ratio are

$$
\frac{1879\ \text{kW}}{243.6\ \text{kg}} = 7.7\ \text{kW/kg}, \qquad \frac{31.15\ \text{kN}}{623.5\ \text{kg} \times g} = 5.1,
$$

so **the turbofan is the heavier of the two systems for the job it does**, carrying the aircraft only in the one flight phase that a proprotor cannot reach. **And the turbofan alone could not lift the aircraft.** Its static thrust is a fraction of the weight at both ends of the solicitation's range,

$$
\frac{F_0}{W} = \frac{31.15\ \text{kN}}{66.72\ \text{kN}} = 0.47 \quad \text{at 15,000 lb}, \qquad \frac{31.15\ \text{kN}}{35.59\ \text{kN}} = 0.88 \quad \text{at 8,000 lb},
$$

so even a jet vectored downward could not hover the X-76, which is why the hover must be the rotors' and why the conversion is a hand-over between two systems rather than a change of attitude of one.

## The Concept's History, Which Is Older Than the Programme

**Bell's July 2025 release calls the X-76 "a brand-new aircraft with the first-ever stop/fold technology"** \[[Bell to build the SPRINT X-plane][ref_bell_phase2]\]. **The aircraft is new, and the idea is fifty-six years older in the open literature**, and the programme's own design choices make most sense read against what that literature found.

### Bell Stopped and Folded a Full-Scale Rotor in 1972

**The configuration was studied as a family of aircraft from the late 1960s.** Engle and Sambell's 1969 paper on folding-proprotor performance describes "moderate disc loading for efficient vertical lift and cruise speeds of 400 knots and beyond" \[[Engle and Sambell 1969][research_engle_sambell_1969]\]. DeTore and Gaffey's paper on the stopped-rotor variant of the proprotor aircraft was presented in 1969 and published in 1970 \[[Detore and Gaffey 1969][research_detore_gaffey_1969]\] \[[DeTore and Gaffey 1970][research_detore_gaffey_1970]\], and a paper on the stability and control of a tilt-fold-proprotor aircraft followed the same path in 1970 and 1971 \[[Nicholson and Tiller 1970][research_nicholson_tiller_1970]\] \[[Tiller and Nicholson 1971][research_tiller_nicholson_1971]\], all cited here for their titles only. **A wind tunnel test of the conversion process of a folding tilt rotor, using an unpowered semispan model, is also from 1971** \[[Magee and Taylor 1971][research_magee_taylor_1971]\], again cited for its title.

**In February 1972 a folding version of Bell's 25-foot proprotor was tested in the 40 by 80 foot wind tunnel at Ames Research Center**, in a programme jointly funded by the National Aeronautics and Space Administration, the Army and the Air Force with Bell as hardware contractor. The XV-15's official history records that the stop-fold tilt rotor "eliminated the rotor/pylon/wing aeroelastic instability by stopping the rotor while in the airplane configuration", that the blades were folded back along the nacelle to cut their drag, and that "a convertible engine was used to produce the jet thrust" \[[The History of the XV-15 Tilt Rotor Research Aircraft][ref_xv15_history]\]. **The test report says the rotor completed more than forty start and stop sequences and made smooth transitions in about two seconds at tunnel speeds up to 175 knots** \[[Report CR-114464 1972][research_reportcr114464_1972]\].

**And the history records why the idea was then shelved.** The stop-fold tilt rotor "had the additional penalties of the increased complexity and increased weight of the stop/fold mechanism, and, with the lack of a developed convertible engine, it was put aside as a potentially feasible concept that would require further advancements to be an effective contender" \[[The History of the XV-15 Tilt Rotor Research Aircraft][ref_xv15_history]\]. **Two of the three obstacles named in that sentence are obstacles the X-76 accepts, weight and complexity, and the third is one it goes around.**

### The Convertible Engine Was the Missing Piece and the X-76 Does Without It

**For two decades after 1972 the folding tiltrotor's literature was largely a propulsion literature**, because a single engine that could drive proprotors in hover and produce jet thrust in cruise was what the concept was thought to need. **DARPA was in it from the start.** A DARPA and National Aeronautics and Space Administration programme to demonstrate a convertible turbofan-turboshaft engine began on 13 May 1981, using a modified TF34-GE-400, for an engine able to power a rotorcraft "in vertical flight and in horizontal flight up to approximately .85 Mach number" \[[Bellin and Brooks 1983][research_bellin_brooks_1983]\]. **A study of the same period found that the fold tilt rotor needed a convertible engine giving thrust to Mach 0.75 and shaft power to proprotors in hover**, and identified a torque converter as the coupling that would let the fan be engaged and disengaged smoothly \[[Gill and Sauer 1983][research_gill_sauer_1983]\].

**The engine was built and run.** A report of 1988 describes a convertible engine with variable inlet guide vanes that ran stably and smoothly in turbofan, turboshaft and dual modes, the last combining fan and shaft power, with fuel consumption in turboshaft mode higher than a dedicated turboshaft's because of fan-tip churning \[[McArdle 1988][research_mcardle_1988]\]. **Its conclusion is the one most directly relevant to the X-76.** Its revised mission analyses for a 400-knot rotorcraft concluded that convertible engines, rather than separate lift and cruise engines, would give a smaller, lighter craft with lower fuel use and lower direct operating cost.

**The X-76 uses separate lift and cruise engines, the configuration that analysis judged heavier.** The solicitation explains why, as far as a document can. It required the demonstrator to "be based on existing engine(s)/propulsion system", allowed only minor upgrades without core changes, and gave its reason, "to ensure the engine or propulsion system development does not delay the overall X-Plane flight demonstration" \[[SPRINT broad agency announcement][ref_sprint_baa]\]. **No convertible engine of this class is in production in any record read here, so the rule excluded the configuration the earlier literature preferred.** **The competitor's documents support this reading.** Aurora's Phase 1B design was a fan-in-wing aircraft, a configuration unlike Bell's in every structural respect, and it too carried "off-the-shelf turbofan and turboshaft engines" \[[Aurora on its SPRINT design, October 2024][ref_aurora_oct24]\], so both finalists answered the rule the same way. The engine mass fraction derived above is the price, and the solicitation accepted it for schedule. That causal reading is this article's inference, and the solicitation also states that the operational system "may include an engine/propulsion system that is envisioned in the future", which leaves the convertible engine open for the aircraft the X-76 is meant to inform.

### Folding Tiltrotor Demonstrators Were Proposed in 1985 and 1986

**A folding tiltrotor demonstrator was proposed twice in the mid-1980s and never built.** Gleiter's 1985 paper argued that the folding tiltrotor offered advantages "for missions requiring long endurance combined with a speed capability in excess of 400 knots", reported that development had begun in 1967 with "no significant technical obstacles", and proposed a demonstrator on either the V-22 or the S-3A powered by the convertible CTFS-34 engine \[[Gleiter 1985][research_gleiter_1985]\]. **A study a year later at the National Aeronautics and Space Administration considered three demonstrator candidates**, the V-22 with a convertible TF34, a modified S-3A with the same engines, and the XV-15 with a conceptual turbofan coupled through a torque converter \[[Eisenberg and Bowles 1986][research_eisenberg_bowles_1986]\]. **Every one of those proposals rested on a convertible engine.** The X-76 is the first folding tiltrotor demonstrator in the records read here to be funded through to manufacture, and it is being built without one.

### DARPA Paid for a Stop-Fold Study in 2008

**The federal award record holds a DARPA contract titled "STOP FOLD TILT ROTOR STUDY"**, awarded on 30 May 2008 to the Bell Boeing Joint Project Office for 584,557.50 dollars on a cost-sharing basis, extended at no cost to 31 March 2009, with one offer received \[[Award HR001108C0077 for a stop fold tilt rotor study][ref_award_stopfold_2008]\]. **No report from that study was found.** It sits between the 1980s demonstrator proposals and Bell's earliest stop-fold patent filings, and it shows that the agency now sponsoring the X-76 had paid for the configuration's study fifteen years before its solicitation.

**Bell's patent record dates its own sustained work.** The earliest Bell filing found on in-flight blade folding has a priority date of 15 June 2010 and describes a foldable rotor system with a blade fold actuator and a swashplate that changes blade pitch \[[CA 2802389][ref_pat_ca2802389]\]. **A cluster of filings with a priority date of 2 December 2014 describes the transition mechanism itself**, a gimbal lock that enables and disables the rotor's gimballing freedom and a blade stop assembly whose arms extend in non-rotary flight \[[US 10,526,068][ref_pat_us10526068]\], a cam that locks the gimbal and the blade cuff together \[[US 10,336,447][ref_pat_us10336447]\], and a fold mechanism driven through the swashplate and pitch links \[[US 11,235,858][ref_pat_us11235858]\]. **That is consistent with Bell's July 2025 statement that it had been working towards the stop-fold aircraft for over 10 years.**

## Dependent Systems

**Every subsystem below is described from a certificate, a solicitation, a patent or a contractor's paper, and none from a description of the X-76 itself**, because none is public.

### The Turboshafts

**The CT7-8 was approved by the Federal Aviation Administration on 29 September 2000 under type certificate E8NE** \[[New Zealand type acceptance report for the CT7 series][ref_nz_ct7]\], General Electric announcing the certificate on 10 October 2000 for an engine selected for the 25,000-pound-class Sikorsky S-92 \[[General Electric on the CT7-8 certificate, 2000][ref_ge_ct7_2000]\]. **The European Union Aviation Safety Agency certificated it on 20 January 2003**, with its basis in that American certificate, and the data sheet gives its output shaft speed as 21,945 revolutions per minute at its principal ratings \[[Type-certificate data sheet for the CT7 series][ref_easa_ct7]\]. **That speed fixes the size of the reduction the drive system must make**, since a proprotor of radius $r$ turning at tip speed $V\_t$ runs at

$$
N_r = \frac{60\, V_t}{2 \pi r}, \qquad \mathcal{G} = \frac{N_e}{N_r} = \frac{2 \pi r\, N_e}{60\, V_t}.
$$

At the XV-15's own rotor speeds the reduction would be 37.3 in hover and 42.4 in airplane mode. **For a 15,000 pound X-76 at the XV-15's hover tip speed of 235 metres per second**, the rotor radii of the hover table give a proprotor speed of 547 revolutions per minute and a reduction of 40.1 at the XV-15's disk loading, 674 and 32.6 at a disk loading of 20, and 1,167 and 18.8 at a disk loading of 60. **A small stowable rotor needs a smaller reduction, which is a weight saving in the gearbox partly offsetting the weight of the fold**, and like every statement here about the X-76's rotor it depends on a disk loading that is not public. **The data sheet read is issue 10, dated 19 December 2025, four days after this article's date**, and the base model's ratings and mass quoted here are those of that issue. **A turboshaft of this family is a natural choice for the solicitation's existing-engine rule**, being the commercial counterpart of the T700 engines that power most of the register's helicopters. **Whether the X-76's two turboshafts run in cruise, idle, or shut down is not public**, and the answer matters for fuel, for restart reliability before the rotors are unfolded, and for the thermal management system the budget book names.

### The Turbofan, and a Certificate That Requires Two of It

**The PW308C's primary certificate is Transport Canada's E-31**, which the New Zealand regulator validated on 18 October 2007 \[[New Zealand type acceptance report for the PW308 series][ref_nz_pw308]\], and the European data sheet read here is that agency's validation. **That sheet states that "the PW308A and PW308C engines are approved for multiple engine installation only"** \[[Type-certificate data sheet for the PW308 series][ref_easa_pw308]\]. It also records that the PW308C is normally used at a reduced take-off thrust of 3,113 decanewtons with an automatic increase to its full rating if the other engine fails. **Both are features of a certificate written for a twin-engine business jet.** If the X-76 carries one PW308C, as the register cell read literally and the renderings suggest, **its installation lies outside the assumption on which the civil certificate rests.** That is not a defect, because the X-76 is an experimental aircraft flown under a DARPA flight release and not a civil type, and DARPA told proposers that engine certification "whether commercial or military does not matter as long as certification documents are available to substantiate whatever airworthiness method is chosen" \[[SPRINT questions and answers][ref_sprint_qa]\]. **But it means the engine's certification basis does not cover its installation, and whatever the civil certificate takes for granted about a second engine has to be substantiated another way.** This is an inference from the data sheet and the cell, and the number of turbofans fitted is not stated in any document read here.

### The Drive System, Which the Patents Describe and the Programme Does Not

**The budget book's subsystem list names a propulsion system, a thermal management system, hydraulics and an electrical system, and no drive system** \[[DARPA budget justification, fiscal year 2026][ref_darpa_pb2026]\]. **Bell's patents describe several.** One couples one or more rotor systems and one or more thrust fans through a combined gearbox with clutches, so that rotors and fans can be engaged in sequence through the transition \[[US 11,577,831][ref_pat_us11577831]\]. One lets a normally idle lift engine drive a pylon-mounted ducted fan for extra thrust once the blades are folded \[[US 11,325,719][ref_pat_us11325719]\]. One routes drive shafts through the wing to the rotor on the pylon \[[US 2023/0331393][ref_pat_us20230331393]\]. **A patent describes an embodiment and claims a right, and it does not say what was built.** **Whether the X-76 has a drive system of either kind is not public.**

### The Fold Mechanism

**The transition has more locked states than a conventional tiltrotor has**, and Bell's filings describe each lock. A rotor-blade assembly in a 2023 filing has "a lock system to enable and disable a folding degree of freedom and a pitching degree of freedom" \[[US 2025/0100674][ref_pat_us20250100674]\], and a companion filing describes a blade position control system that selectively restricts pitch and fold through a multilobe component moved by an actuator \[[US 2025/0100680][ref_pat_us20250100680]\]. **A 2018 method folds the blades of a tiltrotor from an inverted-Y rotor position**, unlocking and restraining each blade in turn \[[US 10,858,094][ref_pat_us10858094]\], and a 2019 filing clamps the stowed blade pneumatically along the pylon \[[US 2021/0078695][ref_pat_us20210078695]\]. **A 2020 filing shapes the blade tip to cut drag when folded** \[[US 11,643,195][ref_pat_us11643195]\], and a 2020 filing applies the stop-fold rotor to a tail-sitter instead of a tiltrotor \[[US 2021/0253239][ref_pat_us20210253239]\]. **Taken together the filings show a decade of work on the locks, the stow and the folded drag**, which are the three things the 1972 test showed were possible and did not make light.

### Control Through Conversion

**The solicitation's flight test checkouts require the transition in both directions to be controlled at maximum design gross weight**, with handling qualities to Level 1 desired and Level 2 required by the military flying qualities handbook and hover manoeuvres judged against the rotorcraft handling qualities specification's mission task elements \[[SPRINT proposers day slides][ref_sprint_overview]\], those being MIL-HDBK-1797 \[[MIL-HDBK-1797, Flying Qualities of Piloted Aircraft][ref_mil_hdbk_1797]\] and ADS-33E-PRF \[[ADS-33E-PRF, Handling Qualities Requirements for Military Rotorcraft][ref_ads_33e]\]. **The slides write the latter as "ADS-PRF-33"**, which this article reads as the same Army specification. The airworthiness basis is MIL-HDBK-516C \[[MIL-HDBK-516C, Airworthiness Certification Criteria][ref_mil_hdbk_516c]\]. **Bell's paper on the sled test describes fly-by-wire transition controls that automatically manage each step of the transition from proprotor flight to jet flight and back** \[[Schank et al 2025][research_schank_xin_2025]\]. **The conversion of a stop-fold aircraft is a sequence of discrete events**, feathering, stopping, indexing, locking and folding, and its reverse, each of which changes the aircraft's mass distribution and drag. That is a control problem for software and not for a pilot's hands, which is consistent with an uncrewed demonstrator, as an inference.

**The structural guidance is modest because the demonstrator is not a fighter.** The solicitation asks for load margins from minus 0.5 to plus 2 g, a 500-hour design life, and a 12 feet per second sink rate at landing without damage. **The fiscal year 2027 book adds a structural proof load test to verify that the built aircraft matches its structural design predictions** \[[DARPA budget justification, fiscal year 2027][ref_darpa_pb2027]\], which is outside this article's date.

## Bell's Designation History, Which the Contractor Overstates

**Bell's March 2026 release says the company "has pushed the known boundaries of flight through pioneering high speed and vertical lift aircraft from the X-1 to the XV-3 and XV-15"** \[[Bell on the X-76][ref_bell_x76]\], and its Phase 1A release of November 2023 called the X-1 and the XV-15 "memorable X-planes" \[[Bell selected for SPRINT Phase 1A][ref_bell_phase1a]\]. **The XV-3 and XV-15 are not research designations.** They belong to the convertiplane sequence, mission letter V with the experimental status prefix X, and the designating instruction says a design number "identifies major design changes within the same basic mission" and stands to the right of "the basic mission or vehicle type symbols" \[[Department of the Air Force Instruction 16-401, 2020][ref_dafi_16_401_2020]\]. **Under that rule, which the [X-67][related_post_a364_x67_slot_taken_by_xq67] article examined, the V sequence is a different count from the research series.** **Bell's research designations are eight**, the [X-1][related_post_a298_bell_x1], [X-2][related_post_a299_bell_x2], [X-5][related_post_a302_bell_x5], [X-9][related_post_a306_bell_x9], [X-14][related_post_a311_bell_x14], [X-16][related_post_a313_bell_x16], [X-22][related_post_a319_bell_x22] and now the X-76. **The X-76 is Bell's first research designation since the X-22**, and its only research designation for a rotorcraft that converts by stopping its rotors. The releases' framing is a heritage claim, and on its own terms a fair one, since the XV-3 made the first full in-flight conversion from helicopter to airplane mode on 18 December 1958 \[[The History of the XV-15 Tilt Rotor Research Aircraft][ref_xv15_history]\]. **It is recorded here only because it uses X-plane for a designation the series does not count.**

## The Flight Test Record, Which Is Empty at This Article's Date

**Nothing has flown.** At this article's date Bell had been selected for Phase 2, which the solicitation placed after a preliminary design review, the fiscal year 2026 plans placed the programme in critical design, and a DARPA spokesperson had told the press that the agency expected a completed demonstrator in 2027 and flight testing in 2028 \[[Breaking Defense on the Phase 2 selection][ref_bd_phase2]\].

### The Sled Test and the Wind Tunnel Are Ground Tests

**Bell's two risk reduction tests are the only test data the programme has reported, and both are ground tests.** In late 2023 a track-guided test vehicle ran on the 10-mile High Speed Test Track at Holloman Air Force Base, integrating "multi-mode propulsion, folding proprotors, and fly-by-wire transition controls" \[[Schank et al 2025][research_schank_xin_2025]\] \[[Bell awarded funding for SPRINT Phase 1B][ref_bell_phase1b]\]. **Bell's paper on it claims "the first known powered prop-to-jet and jet-to-prop transition"**, which is a narrower and more defensible claim than first-ever stop-fold technology, since the 1972 test stopped and folded a rotor in a tunnel but did not carry a whole powered vehicle through the transition. In 2024 a wind tunnel programme at the National Institute for Aviation Research at Wichita State University "validated the stability and control of the aircraft through the rotor fold and unfold sequence in flight" \[[Bell completes stop-fold wind tunnel testing][ref_bell_windtunnel]\], the release being dated 4 December 2024. **Neither result is published in numbers in any document read here.**

### What Would Be Compared With What

**When the X-76 flies, four of this article's derivations become testable.** Its hover gross weight and rotor diameter fix its disk loading, which can be set against the hover envelope above.
