Its demonstrated cruise speed and altitude, with its weight, fix a required lift-to-drag ratio, and its level-flight thrust setting, if published, fixes its equivalent drag area against the ceiling of 0.60 to 0.84 square metres at 400 knots. **Its conversion speed can be set against the band from wing stall to about 175 knots** that the historical tests and the stall relation together suggest. **And its maximum demonstrated speed can be set against the solicitation's threshold of 400 and objective of 450 knots**, the gap between which is the gap between a proprotor that must be slowed and one that must be stopped.

## What the Data Would Change

**If the X-76 meets its threshold, the first thing it settles is not speed but the conversion.** That a stop-fold aircraft can hover and that a jet can push a clean airframe to 400 knots are both established separately. **What no record read here shows in flight is the sequence between them**, with a real wing, a real jet, and blades being stopped and folded in a real airstream at the low end of the conversion band, where the stopped-blade instabilities of the 1960s tests were found. **A clean conversion in both directions would retire the risk that shelved the concept in 1972 other than the convertible engine**, and the convertible engine would then become the remaining argument for the operational aircraft.

**If it meets the objective of 450 knots, it would show that the folded configuration is clean enough to fit the drag budget a single business-jet turbofan sets**, which is the quantitative claim this article makes about the configuration and the one most sensitive to how well the folded blades are faired.

**What it may fail to influence is the procurement it is meant to inform.** The budget books' transition partner moved from service members to services to a component, and **Special Operations Command, the joint partner on every DARPA page, is named as transition partner in none of them.** The literature records two folding tiltrotor demonstrators proposed in the 1980s and neither built, and the configuration's history is one of feasibility repeatedly shown and repeatedly not bought. **The X-76 changes that history only if someone buys what it demonstrates**, and that is a decision no flight test makes.

## Where the Framing Breaks Down

**The aircraft's mass is the unknown that every envelope here is drawn around.** The hover weights, the power fractions, the engine mass fraction and the required lift-to-drag ratio are all functions of a gross weight that is not public, and the inferences drawn from them about where the X-76 lies are inferences.

**The turbofan's lapse is assumed.** The drag-area ceiling is exact in its algebra and approximate in its input, since the factor $\varphi$ is swept rather than known, and a real turbofan's lapse is not a pure function of density. **The altitude independence of the ceiling is exactly as good as that assumption.**

**The hover assumptions are typical rather than measured.** A figure of merit of 0.75, a drive efficiency of 0.90 and a download of 10 percent are reasonable for a tiltrotor, and the X-76's proprotors, which must fold, may be less efficient in hover than an optimised proprotor. **The turboshafts' hot-day lapse is not modelled at all**, so every hot-day hover weight above is high by an unknown amount.

**The proprotor's speed limit is taken from compressibility alone.** Whirl flutter, which the historical record names as the reason the first stop-fold tests stopped the rotor, is cited and not computed, and for a particular aircraft it may bind at a lower speed than the helical tip Mach number does. **The 0.85 limit is an assumption.**

**The register's engine cell is the only official configuration fact**, its plus sign is unexplained, and the turbofan count of one is a reading. With $n\_F$ turbofans the drag ceiling is

$$
f_{\max} = \frac{2\, n_F\, F_0\, \varphi}{\rho_0 V^2},
$$

**so if the X-76 carries two turbofans, the drag budget doubles and the certificate point disappears**, and nothing in any document read here excludes that.

**And the patents describe embodiments of a company's work, not the X-76.** They are cited for what Bell has filed and for the problems those filings address, and no sentence here claims that any patented mechanism is fitted to the aircraft.

## The Contemporary Literature

**There is almost no literature on the X-76 itself, and the one paper on its direct ancestor is the contractor's.** Bell's 2025 paper on the stop-fold sled test is the only technical publication in the sweep about the programme's own hardware \[[Schank et al 2025][research_schank_xin_2025]\]. **What the sweep maps is the large literature the X-76 sits in**, being the stopped and folding rotor, the tiltrotor and its aeroelastic and compressibility limits, high-speed vertical lift as a class, hover physics and the downwash at the surface, conversion between modes, the propulsion families and the convertible engine, rotorcraft conceptual design, the missions the solicitation names, and the practice of research aircraft and their flight test.

**The sweep asked 86 questions of the bibliographic index and 30 of the National Aeronautics and Space Administration's reports server, and the pool was 12,957 records**, 11,585 from the index and 1,372 from the server. **The gate admitted 3,847 of them, and the article cites 3,584 distinct swept works and 17 research primaries chosen by hand, 3,601 in all.** Removing 246 repeat registrations leaves 3,601 distinct admitted works, of which 17 are the hand-chosen primaries' own records, White's 1968 forum paper and its 1969 journal version both standing for one primary. A work admitted to several clusters is cited once, under the first of them in the order below. **The bookkeeping is a set of identities, each recomputed by the verifier**, and the gate's selectivity and the clusters' overlap are two ratios,

$$
\frac{3{,}847}{12{,}957} = 0.297, \qquad \bar{c} = \frac{4{,}594}{3{,}847} = 1.194,
$$

so the gate admitted 29.7 percent of the pool, and an admitted record sits in 1.194 clusters on average.

$$
12{,}957 = 11{,}585 + 1{,}372, \qquad 3{,}601 = 3{,}847 - 246, \qquad 3{,}601 = (3{,}601 - 17) + 17
$$

| Cluster | Works cited | Key |
|---|---:|---|
| Stopped, folded and stowed rotors | 119 | `stop_fold` |
| The tiltrotor and its relatives | 1,255 | `tiltrotor` |
| Proprotor whirl flutter and tiltrotor aeroelasticity | 135 | `whirl_flutter` |
| High-speed propellers and tip compressibility | 146 | `propeller_mach` |
| High-speed vertical lift as a class | 936 | `high_speed_vtol` |
| Hover, wake, downwash and the surface | 307 | `hover_downwash` |
| Conversion between hover and forward flight | 49 | `transition` |
| Turboshafts, turbofans and the convertible engine | 414 | `propulsion` |
| Rotorcraft conceptual design and sizing | 80 | `design_sizing` |
| The missions the solicitation names | 55 | `mission` |
| Research aircraft and their flight test | 105 | `demonstrator` |
| **All clusters** | **3,601** | |

**The clusters are uneven and the unevenness is informative.** The tiltrotor literature is the largest, the stopped and folding rotor one of the smaller, and the transition literature small and recent, much of it about small electric aircraft. **The configuration the X-76 embodies has a literature concentrated in the late 1960s through the 1980s and then a gap**, the folding tiltrotor's papers thinning once the convertible engine programmes ended, and a revival in the 2020s led by the high-speed vertical take-off and landing work this programme belongs to \[[Young 2024][research_young_2024]\].

### What the Literature Establishes, and Where It Is Moving

**Read as a whole rather than cluster by cluster, the literature makes four points that bear on the X-76, and it is silent on a fifth.**

**First, the stopped rotor has an experimental base from the 1960s and 1970s and a revival in the 2020s, and the revival is divided.** The early work found that a stopped blade can go violently unstable at some combinations of shaft and pitch angle \[[White 1969][research_white_1969]\], and that a full-scale folding proprotor could be stopped and started in about two seconds \[[Report CR-114464 1972][research_reportcr114464_1972]\]. **The contemporary reassessment by Brown and Ahuja reviews why the historical stopped-rotor experiments were not successful and argues that "with modern technologies, materials, and analysis tools" a low disc-loading rotor can be used "not only for efficient VTOL but also for efficient cruise"** \[[Brown and Ahuja 2023][research_brown_ahuja_2023]\], where VTOL is vertical take-off and landing. Young's survey of novel concepts for flight at 400 knots or more, written at the National Aeronautics and Space Administration, discusses "two stopped-rotor concepts and two non-stopped-rotor concepts" \[[Young 2024][research_young_2024]\]. **So the current literature splits between keeping the stopped rotor in the airstream as a lifting surface and removing it from the airstream altogether**, and the X-76 belongs to the second branch, as Bell's own paper on the sled test states \[[Schank et al 2025][research_schank_xin_2025]\]. Work on the flight dynamics of stopped-rotor aircraft continues at small scale \[[He et al 2022][research_he_gao_2022]\], cited for its title.

**Second, whirl flutter is the most active experimental subject in the whole survey, and every result in it is about making a turning proprotor survive at higher speed.** The Maryland Tiltrotor Rig is a Froude-scaled, 4.75-foot, semispan flutter rig first tested to 100 knots in the Navy's Carderock tunnel \[[Tsai et al 2024][research_tsai_sutherland_2024]\]. Its swept-tip blades, swept 20 degrees aft from 80 percent of radius, showed "higher wing chord damping using swept-tip blades in freewheel conditions" \[[Sutherland et al 2024][research_sutherland_tsai_2024]\], and the comprehensive analysis matched wing beam damping well while underpredicting chord damping and missing the effect of powered operation \[[Gul and Datta 2024][research_gul_datta_2024]\]. **Run to 200 knots in freewheeling airplane mode, the model "remained flutter-free up to 200 knots", but its "chord damping showed signs of a sudden drop after 175 knots"** \[[Akinwale and Datta 2023][research_akinwale_datta_2023]\], and a 2026 analysis of the same rig's free decays found that damping falls with response amplitude, "a trend missed by conventional linear estimation methods" \[[Simmons and Riso 2026][research_simmons_riso_2026]\]. **The European Advanced Testbed for Tiltrotor Aeroelastics, called ATTILA, a Froude-scale semispan model of a civil tiltrotor's wing and proprotor, found the wing torsion mode "displaying a sharp trend towards negative damping at higher speeds"** and an unexpected contribution from stiction in the blade pitch mechanism \[[van 't Hoff et al 2024][research_vanthoff_kapteijn_2024]\], which a 2026 analysis could reproduce only by modelling friction in the joints \[[De Vita et al 2026][research_devita_cassoni_2026]\]. **A design study found that replacing one large wingtip rotor with two of half the disk area "pushed the instability speed by more than 100%"** \[[Gul and Yeo 2025][research_gul_yeo_2025]\]. The standing reviews are Reed's of 1965 \[[Reed 1965][research_reed_1965]\] and Kunz's of 2002 and 2005 \[[Kunz 2002][research_kunz_2002]\] \[[Kunz 2005][research_kunz_2005]\], the latter cited for their titles. **This literature is the measure of what the X-76 avoids.** The stop-fold configuration's premise is that removing the rotor from the airstream is easier than making it stable there, and a body of current work in which a scaled rig is flutter-free to 200 knots but losing chord damping above 175 is the evidence for that premise. Scaled speeds do not transfer to full-scale speeds without the rig's scaling laws, and this article does not attempt the transfer.

**Third, the convertible engine, the missing piece of 1972, has had no new literature in the survey since the 1990s.** The DARPA and National Aeronautics and Space Administration programme of 1981 \[[Bellin and Brooks 1983][research_bellin_brooks_1983]\], the 1988 stand test and mission analysis \[[McArdle 1988][research_mcardle_1988]\] and a 1996 report on the engine's dynamic and transient performance \[[McArdle et al 1996][research_mcardle_barth_1996]\], cited for its title, are the field's last substantial entries in this pool. **Both SPRINT finalists then answered the existing-engine rule with separate engines**, so the literature's preferred solution is the one the current programme could not use.

**Fourth, the hover side of runway independence rests on measurements that are decades old.** The full-scale tiltrotor hover data are the Ames tests of the 1980s \[[Felker and Betzina 1985][research_felker_betzina_1985]\] \[[Felker et al 1986][research_felker_maisel_1986]\], and a 2017 report set out to widen that experimental base because computational work had concentrated on only two successful tiltrotors, the XV-15 and the MV-22B \[[Harris 2017][research_harris_2017]\]. The study most directly about people standing in a rotor's outwash \[[Calvert and Wenren 2019][research_calvert_wenren_2019_2]\] is cited for its title. **The contemporary high-speed vertical take-off and landing design studies, including a 2024 preliminary design study and a student design competition winner, are cited for their titles** \[[Yang et al 2024][research_yang_coxgross_2024]\] \[[O'Brien and Delgado 2024][research_obrien_delgado_2024]\].

**And the literature is silent on the X-76's keystone.** No work in the survey reports a stop-fold conversion in flight. Bell's sled test is the only powered transition of a whole vehicle in the record, and it was on rails \[[Schank et al 2025][research_schank_xin_2025]\]. **The flight test that would fill that gap is the X-76's, and it lies after this article's date.**

### Stopped, folded and stowed rotors

**This is the X-76's own lineage.** It holds the folding-proprotor studies of the late 1960s and 1970s, the full-scale folding rotor test of 1972, the stopped-rotor and rotor/wing concepts including the [X-50][related_post_a347_boeing_x50]'s canard rotor/wing, the X-wing's stopped-rotor dynamics, and the stopped-rotor revival of the 2020s.

### The tiltrotor and its relatives

**The largest cluster, and the configuration the X-76 is in hover and conversion.** It runs from the XV-3 and XV-15 research programmes through the V-22 and the civil tiltrotor studies to tilt-wing and electric tiltrotor work, and much of it is the National Aeronautics and Space Administration's.

### Hover, wake, downwash and the surface

**Runway independence is a question about the air below the aircraft**, and this cluster holds rotor hover performance, rotor wakes in and out of ground effect, brownout, and the effect of outwash on people and objects nearby.

### Turboshafts, turbofans and the convertible engine

**The engine families the register names, and the engine the X-76 does without.** It holds the turboshaft and T700 literature, rotorcraft drive systems and variable-speed rotors, turbofan installed performance, and the convertible fan/shaft engine programmes of the 1970s and 1980s.

## The Source Base

**This article rests on a broad base of government primary documents, and almost all of them are about the programme rather than the aircraft.** The solicitation, its slides and its questions and answers were read from the contracting portal \[[SPRINT broad agency announcement][ref_sprint_baa]\] \[[SPRINT proposers day slides][ref_sprint_overview]\] \[[SPRINT questions and answers][ref_sprint_qa]\]. Four budget justification books were read in full for the programme's entries. **The award record was read by contract and by transaction**, with three first-phase contracts and the 2008 study found and Bell's instruments not found. **Aurora's two releases of 2024 were read for the competitor's propulsion and schedule.** The designating instruction of 2020 was read for its definition of a design number, and the three flight-standard documents the solicitation names were identified from their catalogue entries and not read. **DARPA's programme page was read at its current version and as archived on 24 December 2024, 12 May 2025, 10 July 2025 and 12 December 2025** \[[DARPA SPRINT programme page, as archived on 12 May 2025][ref_darpa_sprint_wb_2025_05]\] \[[DARPA SPRINT programme page, as archived on 10 July 2025][ref_darpa_sprint_wb_2025_07]\], the last three days before this article's date, and every statement about the programme as it stood at the date rests on that capture.

**Apart from DARPA's picture, the engine certification documents are the only primary documents about the aircraft's hardware.** They are the European Union Aviation Safety Agency's data sheets and the New Zealand regulator's two type acceptance reports, the latter dated 2007 and 2020 and so inside this article's date, read because the Federal Aviation Administration's and Transport Canada's own sheets were not retrieved, together with General Electric's certification releases of 2000 and 2004. **The New Zealand report on the PW308 gives its take-off rating in pounds and agrees with the European sheet's kilonewtons to within a pound**, which is the only cross-check of a rating this article could make between two regulators. DARPA's artist's concept of the X-76 was read as an image, and the reading is stated as a reading of a picture. **The register is the only official document naming the engines**, read at its current version and at the three archived copies that bracket the row's first appearance.

**Bell's releases were read from Bell's newsroom and, for two of them, from Textron's investor-relations mirror**, which is how the two wrong datelines were found. **Fourteen of Bell's patent publications were read for their abstracts** and are cited for nothing their abstracts do not say. **The National Aeronautics and Space Administration history of the XV-15 was read for its account of the 1972 folding rotor test, its appendix specifications and its chronology**, and seventeen research primaries were read for their abstracts, and one of them, the 1985 XV-15 rotor hover test, in part for its text, each fetched by identifier and checked against a probe phrase from its title.

**The reports server is the right server for this subject, unlike the last article's.** It contributed 616 of the 3,584 swept works, 17.2 percent, because the folding tiltrotor, the convertible engine and the tiltrotor's aeroelastic research were sponsored and largely written at the National Aeronautics and Space Administration and its Army partner. **The admitted works run from 1928 to 2027 with a median of 2009**, the latest being a record its publisher's metadata dates to 2027. **The gate was audited in both directions.** It carries 32 keep cases and 42 refusal cases, each retested with its spaces replaced by hyphens, and the refusal cases were taken from what the qualified queries returned. **Three rounds of reading admitted samples found the homonyms that the qualified queries still let in**, medical papers on austere environments, power-grid brownouts, glacial outwash, convertible bonds, electric variable-speed drives, land-use conversion corridors, molecular propellers and agricultural spraying drones, and each became a refusal case before the counts above were taken.

**One document the article would most like to have read could not be.** Bell's Phase 1 and Phase 2 instruments, presumably other transactions, are not in the award record, so the value of the contract that is building the X-76 is not known to this article.

## Epistemic State

### The Article's Own Vantage Point

**The article is dated 15 December 2025 and written on 2 October 2026.** The allocation of the designation happened inside its dateline, 56 days before it. **The designation's publication did not**, the register first showing the row between 15 January and 1 February 2026 and DARPA announcing it on 9 March 2026. Everything this article says about the programme from government documents of March 2023 through June 2025 was public at its date. **Everything it says about the aircraft's name, critical design review and current schedule was not.**

### Historical Fact, From Primary Documents

- The X-76A was allocated on 20 October 2025 to Bell Textron, sponsor DARPA, with the engine cell `2 GE CT7-8, PW308C+` and a description marked as the compiler's.
- DARPA posted solicitation HR001123S0031 in March 2023 with the demonstrator guidance, mission and engine rule as quoted, a 42-month goal to first flight, and a Phase 1 budget of 75 million dollars.
- Three first-phase contracts are in the award record with the dates, values and offer count stated, and a 2008 DARPA stop-fold tilt rotor study to the Bell Boeing Joint Project Office is too.
- The budget books carry the figures, wording and plans as tabulated and quoted.
- The CT7-8 and PW308C ratings and masses are as their data sheets state, and the PW308C is approved for multiple-engine installation only.
- A folding 25-foot proprotor was stopped and started more than forty times in a full-scale wind tunnel test in 1972, and the concept was then set aside for weight, complexity and the lack of a developed convertible engine.
- DARPA and the National Aeronautics and Space Administration funded a convertible turbofan-turboshaft engine programme beginning on 13 May 1981.
- Both Phase 1B designs carried existing turbofan and turboshaft engines, Aurora's by its own release of October 2024.
- The CT7-8 was approved by the Federal Aviation Administration on 29 September 2000, and the PW308C's primary certificate is Transport Canada's E-31.

### Measured From the Documents Rather Than Asserted by Them

- The row's first public appearance, between 15 January and 1 February 2026, by archive capture.
- The absence of any Bell instrument from the award record under the searches described.
- The three-month disagreement among DARPA's and Bell's statements on when Phase 2 began.
- The one-year dateline errors on Textron's mirror of two releases.
- The count of 23 other register rows powered by T700 or CT7 engines and the absence of any other turboshaft and turbofan pairing.

### Engineering Analysis, Derived Here and Checkable

- The hover envelope of two CT7-8s across disk loading, at the stated figure of merit, drive efficiency and download.
- The drag-area ceiling $f\_{\max} = 2 F\_0 \varphi / (\rho\_0 V^2)$ and its independence of altitude under the stated lapse model.
- The required lift-to-drag ratios across the solicitation's weight and altitude bands.
- The helical tip Mach numbers at the XV-15's tip speed and the tip speed reductions needed to hold 0.85.
- The engine mass fractions of 0.163 and 0.306 at the solicitation's weight limits.
- The schedule slip of at least eight months against the solicitation's goal.

### Inference, Stated as Such

- That the X-76 is heavier than 15,000 pounds or near it, from the hover power margin and the engine mass fraction.
- That it hovers at a disk loading well above a conventional tiltrotor's, from the stowed-blade geometry and the absence of a disk loading limit.
- That it carries one turbofan, from the register cell read literally and the renderings.
- That it is uncrewed, from the compiler's description, the solicitation's flight termination clause and the budget book's ground station.
- That Bell's instruments are other transactions, from the solicitation's permission and the award record's silence.
- That the existing-engine rule is why the X-76 has separate lift and cruise engines rather than the convertible engine the earlier literature preferred, a reading the competitor's identical choice supports.

### What the Record Does Not Settle

**What the plus sign after the turbofan means.** How many turbofans are fitted. The aircraft's gross weight, rotor diameter, wing area and drag. Whether the turboshafts run in cruise. **Whether any patented drive or fold mechanism is fitted.** The value of Bell's contract. **And the keystone itself, since no stop-fold aircraft has converted in flight in any record read here.**

### Statements in This Article That Postdate Its Own Date

- The register's publication of the X-76A row, between 15 January and 1 February 2026.
- DARPA's and Bell's announcements of 9 March 2026, with the designation, the critical design review, the programme manager's rank, the 1776 statement and the plan for flight testing in early 2028.
- DARPA's programme page in its current form, and DARPA's artist's concept of the X-76, whose single exhaust and absent cockpit glazing are read here.
- The fiscal year 2027 budget book of April 2026, its figures for fiscal years 2025 through 2027, and its plans, each marked where used.
- The CT7 data sheet's issue 10, dated 19 December 2025, four days after the date, whose base-model ratings are used.
- The defence journalism of March 2026 \[[Air and Space Forces on the X-76][ref_afa_x76]\] \[[The War Zone on the X-76][ref_twz_x76]\] \[[The Aviationist on the X-76][ref_aviationist_x76]\].
- **The award record, read in October 2026**, whose totals include modifications through August 2025 and are therefore current totals rather than totals as they stood at any one earlier date.
- The literature sweep, run from current indexes and including records dated to 2026 and one dated to 2027 by its publisher's metadata.

## Out of Scope

**The designation's number.** Why this aircraft is the X-76 and not the X-69 is the [X-69 through X-75][related_post_a366_x69_through_x75_leapfrogged_block] article's subject and is not reargued here.

**The operational aircraft.** The solicitation's operational system, its 5,000 pound payload and its cargo bay are described because they bound the demonstrator, and the operational aircraft's own sizing, which would need a mission fuel analysis, is not attempted.

**Aeroelastic analysis.** Whirl flutter and the stopped-blade instabilities are cited as the reasons the configuration exists and as risks to its conversion, and neither is computed, since both need structural properties that are not public.

**Acoustics and signatures.** The literature on tiltrotor noise is in the survey because it is part of the tiltrotor's literature, and the X-76's own acoustic or other signatures are not treated.

**The competing configurations.** Aurora's Phase 1 design is described here only for its propulsion and schedule, from Aurora's own releases, and the Phase 1A designs of Northrop Grumman and Piasecki are not described in any primary document read here. The comparative literature on high-speed rotorcraft concepts is cited without being used to rank them.

**The procurement that would follow**, which is a question about the services' budgets rather than about an aeroplane.

## Conclusion

**The X-76 is a demonstrator built to answer one question, whether an aircraft can hover over an unprepared surface and then cruise at 400 knots or more, and its answer is to carry two propulsion systems and use them one at a time.** Two CT7-8 turboshafts lift it on proprotors, a PW308C turbofan pushes it once those proprotors have been stopped and folded, and the register's engine cell, the only official configuration fact in the public record, says so in one short line that ends in an unexplained plus sign.

**The certificated ratings of those engines bound the aircraft from both ends without its mass.** At the assumed hover efficiencies, the turboshafts' 3,758 kilowatts would hover about 30,800 pounds at the XV-15's disk loading, twice the solicitation's heaviest guidance, so either the X-76 is heavier than its guidance, or it hovers with a large margin, or its proprotors are small and its disk loading high. **The turbofan's sea-level thrust fixes the largest equivalent drag area the aircraft can carry at 400 knots, 0.60 to 0.84 square metres across the assumed lapse, and the density cancels, so that ceiling is the same at every altitude in the solicitation's band.** The folded configuration has to fit inside it. **And the engines alone are a sixth of the solicitation's heaviest aircraft**, which is the price of carrying two systems.

**The concept is fifty-six years old in the open literature and was shown at full scale in a wind tunnel in 1972**, when a folding 25-foot proprotor was stopped and started more than forty times. It was then set aside for its weight, its complexity, and the lack of a convertible engine that could drive rotors and make jet thrust in one machine. **The 1980s built that engine and proposed folding tiltrotor demonstrators around it, and none was built.** The X-76 accepts the weight and the complexity and goes around the engine, because its solicitation required existing engines so that engine development could not delay first flight. **The earlier literature judged separate lift and cruise engines the heavier choice, and the X-76 makes it anyway.** The solicitation's existing-engine rule explains why, and the competing finalist's identical choice, a turbofan and a turboshaft in a fan-in-wing aircraft, shows that the rule and not the configuration drove it.

**At this article's date nothing had flown, the designation was not public, and the schedule had moved by at least eight months against the solicitation's goal.** The programme's documents disagree on when its second phase began, a contractor's mirror misdates two of its releases by a year, and the contract that is building the aircraft was not found in the public award record. **The question the X-76 exists to answer remains open, and it is the conversion**, because hovering and fast cruise have each been done and the sequence between them, in flight, with a real wing and a real jet, has not.
